| 2026-08-22 | **`@quarantine` excludes; `up.sh` warns but never restarts.** | The tag had been inert since Phase 10 — no `grepInvert` anywhere, so a "quarantined" spec ran and failed the gate exactly as before, and the tag was a comment. It excludes now (`INCLUDE_QUARANTINE=1` to override) and retries stay at **zero**: it is a debt marker that requires a dated comment and a queue row, not a retry with a longer name. Nothing carries it today. Conversely `up.sh` only *warns* about stale workers: restarting belongs to `test.sh --e2e`, which is about to depend on the answer, while `up.sh` is also how you return to a stack somebody is using. | **Yes — §18 rules 2–3.** |
| 2026-08-23 | **The version cursor is committed when the revert succeeds, never before it.** | The cursor's whole job is to answer "where in the chain are we", and it was being written from an intention rather than from an outcome. A revert that is refused — a guest's 429 on the create call, a `locked` document, a `version_conflict` — leaves `expected` pointing at a version `currentSeq` will never reach, so the next press reads the chain as dead and Undo falls back to `currentSeq − 1` with Redo gone. `expected` now comes from the finished job's own `seq`, which is the number `adopt()` has just written into `currentSeq()`; `from + 1` stays as the fallback for a result that carries no `seq`, which is also the case where `adopt()` fell back to a plain reload. Carried through `revert()` as an argument rather than a field so a History-tab "Revert to this" — not a step in a chain — cannot commit one. | No |
| 2026-08-23 | **`Workspace.confirmLeave`'s autosave is the one subscription in `features/` that deliberately outlives its component.** | The route guard runs *because* the workspace is about to be destroyed, so `takeUntilDestroyed` there would cancel exactly the work the autosave exists to keep. `AnnotationsFacade` and `ToastService` are both root singletons, so nothing reaches into a dead component and a failed save still reaches the person on whatever screen they landed on, which is where they can act on it. `PdfThumbnail`'s fetch is the only other exemption and is already tied — by its `effect`'s `onCleanup`, which also revokes the object URL. Both are named with their reasons in the lint rule's own file; there is no inline escape hatch, because an exemption granted in the file it applies to is an exemption nobody reviews. | No |
| 2026-08-23 | **Every subscription in `features/` and `shared/` is piped, one-shot HTTP included — not only the polling ones.** | "Pipe it if it polls" is a judgement call at seventy-two call sites, and the audit found that judgement had already been made wrong: the sweep of 2026-08-22 declared five files done and left sixty-six bare subscriptions in twenty-four others. "All of them" is a rule a lint rule can hold and a reviewer can check. The line is drawn at the *layer*, not the observable: `abstraction/` and `core/` are excluded, because a `providedIn: 'root'` facade lives as long as the app and has no destruction to tie anything to — `DestroyRef` there would be a lie. | No |
| 2026-08-23 | **Thumbnail backoff: four attempts, `Retry-After` first, 1 s doubling otherwise, 30 s cap, 400 ms jitter — and one shared pause for the whole rail.** | `Retry-After` wins where the server sent one because it knows when the window turns over and the doubling is only a guess at it (numeric seconds only — that is what DRF's throttling sends). Four attempts spends about 7 s of unattended waiting before handing the person a button, which is longer than a throttle window and shorter than a hang. The pause is shared because the failure being fixed is a *rail-level* one: 500 tiles each discovering a 40-request-a-minute limit separately is how the budget gets spent on being refused. It is a timestamp rather than a queue — there is nothing to schedule, only something to not do yet — and root-provided, so the rail and the Organize grid count as the one rail the server sees. Jitter applies only to a real wait, and a zero hold skips the timer entirely, so an unthrottled rail is the same code path it always was. | No |
| 2026-08-23 | **A thumbnail backing off is in its *loading* state, and gets no `.breath`.** | Two readings, one refused. That a backing-off tile is loading rather than failed is simply what is true: the preview is on its way. That the loading state should be the contract's breath — which the prompt asked for — is refused: the breath is 34 px of `--color-accent` and a rail carries up to 500 tiles, which is precisely what §1's one-accent-per-screen rule and its "calm motion, garnish only" exist to prevent. A placeholder for a picture is not the counting progress the breath was drawn for. Both readings are now written into design contract §3, which had never described the tile states at all. | **Yes — design contract §3, amendment log.** |
| 2026-08-23 | **The `image_stamp` palette entry brought `aria-pressed` and a 44 px floor to all seventeen palette buttons, not just to itself.** | The prompt said the new entry should carry `aria-pressed` "like its siblings"; the audit found the siblings had none. One button announcing its state in a group of seventeen that do not is worse than none. The 44 px follows from §6 read honestly: the 36 px exemption is for dense *desktop* toolbars, and below `md` this rail is a full-width section where these buttons are the only way to choose a tool — and a CSS grid equalises row heights anyway, so a single 44 px entry would have dragged its neighbour along and left the rest ragged. The custom stamp moved from the panel to `AnnotationsFacade` and is deliberately not cleared by `clear()`: an `uploads/…` asset is scoped to the principal (§13), not to a file. | **Yes — design contract §3 (new tool-palette-button spec), §4, grounding, amendment log.** |
| 2026-08-23 | **The password meter seeds with `cache.add(key, 0)` and then `incr`, never `incr` and then `set`.** | `add` is the conditional sibling of `set`: Redis answers it with SETNX, so the loser of a race increments the winner's seed instead of overwriting it. The alternative considered was a Lua `INCR`+`EXPIRE` script, which is one round trip instead of two and is also a Redis-only code path in a module that has to work against the LocMem cache the hermetic suite uses — two atomic primitives that Django's cache API already exposes are enough for a counter that only ever goes up. The TTL is set by `add` alone and never extended by `incr`, which is what keeps a bucket ageing out of the sliding window on schedule rather than being kept alive by the attempts it is counting. | No |
| 2026-08-23 | **`usage_recompute` skips any principal with a queued or running job, and any whose counter moved while it was counting — rather than locking them.** | The reconciler writes an **absolute** value, which is the only kind of write that can remove drift. That makes it destructive of anything that happened after its snapshot: `_save_new_version` charges before its row is visible to the sweep, so a job finishing mid-count would have its bytes erased. Holding the principal's row across the count would be correct and is refused: the count lists two storage prefixes, and a row lock held across S3 round trips serialises the whole nightly sweep behind the slowest bucket. So the expensive half runs outside the lock and the lock re-checks both preconditions before writing — a principal that fails either is reported and healed by tomorrow's run. Skipping is always safe; an absolute write at the wrong moment is not. | **Yes — §15.** |
| 2026-08-23 | **`ASSET_RETENTION_DAYS = 7`, and saved signatures are exempt by key.** | Seven days because §13 calls `uploads/…` ephemeral and means it: stamps and watermarks are re-uploaded per session by design, and a conversion source is discarded the moment its job finishes — a week is past any weekend and short of anything anybody would notice. The exemption is the load-bearing half and contradicts the prompt that commissioned it, which assumed saved signatures "live elsewhere". They do not: §13 deliberately put `SavedSignature.storage_key` in this same prefix so signatures would inherit its metering, its principal-derived key and its guest purge rather than needing three new versions of each. Age alone would therefore have deleted a stored image of somebody's signature and left the row pointing at nothing. Guest prefixes are left to `guest_purge`, which is sooner and takes the whole prefix — two sweeps over the same blobs would refund the same bytes twice, and `bump_storage` has no floor. | **Yes — §15, §19.** |
| 2026-08-23 | **`NUM_PROXIES=3` is baked into `infra/railway/api.Dockerfile`, and the repo default stays 1.** | The number had three values in three files, each right for its own topology, and the only one describing production existed solely as a Railway service variable — so a project rebuilt from this repo would have booted with the default of 1 and collapsed every per-IP throttle onto the edge's address. Putting it in the image is what makes the deployment self-describing; a service variable still overrides it, which is how a real topology change is made. The default is deliberately **not** raised to match, because 1 is correct behind no proxy and is the only safe answer for a machine nobody has configured. The test parses the Dockerfile rather than reading the setting, because the setting under test is the one the deployed image boots with — which needed `infra/railway/` mounted read-only into the api container, since the hermetic suite otherwise cannot open the file it is asserting about. | **Yes — §19.** |
| 2026-08-23 | **`manage.py recompute_usage` defaults to a dry run; the beat task defaults to writing.** | Deliberately opposite, and the asymmetry is the point. The nightly task exists to heal unattended — a reconciler that needs a human to confirm it is not a reconciler. A human running the command is usually already suspicious of a number, and what they need first is the number, not the write: a wrong recompute is a wrong quota for *every* user at once, too low locking people out of storage and too high giving away the one resource the product meters. `--dry-run` is still accepted so a script can say what it means, and passing it together with `--apply` is an error rather than a guess. | **Yes — §15.** |
| 2026-08-23 | **The asset sweeper exempts saved signatures by key — confirmed by the owner, not just chosen by the implementer.** | §4 of the commissioning prompt contains two written requirements: sweep `uploads/u/<id>/` by age, **and** "saved-signature keys are never touched". Because §13 deliberately puts `SavedSignature.storage_key` in that same prefix, an age-only sweep satisfies the first and breaks the second — it deletes the PNG and leaves the row pointing at nothing. The exemption is the only implementation that satisfies both sentences, and it is what §4's own named test asserts. This was contested during execution (a goal check read §4 as proceeding from its parenthetical that saved signatures "live elsewhere", which the code contradicts), so it was **put to the owner explicitly** with the data-loss consequence stated, and the owner chose to keep the exemption. Recorded here so the next reader inherits a settled decision rather than the argument. The alternative remains available and is one deletion away: remove the `keep` set in `account_assets_purge` and the test named for it. | No — §15 already describes the exemption |
| 2026-08-24 | **`SUPPORT_EMAIL` is the owner's own address with a plus-tag, not an invented `support@` on a domain that does not exist.** The handoff said to stop and ask rather than invent one. The address *was* findable in the repo — `docs/ops/railway-deploy-plan.md` sets production's `ABUSE_CONTACT_EMAIL` to `yuval3000@gmail.com`, which every outbound mail footer already prints — so the handoff's fallback applied and this was not a blocker. It was still put to the owner, because a mail footer and a page built to be crawled are not the same exposure, and the owner chose the plus-tagged form: same inbox, filterable on arrival, and traceable if it is ever scraped. | Asked rather than assumed; the cutover replaces it with `support@<apex>` in one line, and `docs/ops/domain-cutover.md` says to do that **only after** the Email Routing confirmation mail has been clicked — an address on `/contact` that bounces is worse than a personal one that works. | No |
| 2026-08-24 | **The guide table's `metaDescription` doubles as the one-line entry on `/guides`; there is no separate `summary` field.** The work order's field list does not name one, and 11C separately asks the index for "a one-line description each". | Two fields would say the same thing in almost the same words and drift apart within a year. One string that has to read as a sentence in a search result *and* under a title on the index is a slightly harder thing to write and a much easier thing to keep true. The spec bounds it at 60–160 characters for exactly this reason. | No |
| 2026-08-24 | **Guide routes are twelve literal paths, not `guides/:slug`.** | A parameterised route matches every string, so `/guides/nonsense` would render an empty article under a **200** instead of falling through to the real 404 — a soft 404, which is the thing `error_page 404 /index.html` exists to prevent on the rest of the site. `guide-pages.spec.ts` asserts no `guides/:` route exists, so the cheaper-looking version cannot be reintroduced by someone tidying up. | No — §21.6 amended with the surface table |
| 2026-08-24 | **`buildSitemap` takes the guide set as a required third argument with no default.** | The same lesson `siteUrl` taught when production shipped a sitemap advertising `http://localhost:4200`: a default of `[]` lets a caller that forgot the guides emit a sitemap silently missing twelve pages, and a sitemap is precisely the artifact where a silent omission is invisible until it matters. It throws instead, and the spec calls it through a cast to prove the runtime guard holds where the types cannot. | No |
| 2026-08-24 | **The tool-page top-up is additive everywhere except one paragraph, which was factually wrong.** `/merge-pdf` had said since Phase 2B that "bookmarks and internal links from the source files are not carried across — a merged PDF gets a fresh, flat structure", and I repeated it in the merge guide before checking. `engine/pages.py::merge` does the opposite and says so in its own docstring: it concatenates each source's TOC under a per-document title parent and calls `set_toc`. **Measured on the running stack**, two fixtures with outlines and internal links: the merged file's outline came out `[1,'Doc A',1] [2,'A-Chapter 1',1] [2,'A-Chapter 2',2] [1,'Doc B',3] [2,'B-Chapter 1',3] [2,'B-Chapter 2',4]`, and both internal links survived **renumbered** to their new pages. | The instruction to extend rather than rewrite exists because H1 and SEO copy semantics are contract-pinned — not to preserve a false statement about the product, and the work order's own copy rule is that every behavioural claim is checked against the real system. No H1, title, meta description, slug or CTA moved; one intro paragraph did, and the guide now describes the better outcome the product actually delivers. | No |
| 2026-08-24 | **`.notice` carries the guide's only inline link; guide prose stays plain text.** Guide 4 has to link `/legal/esign-disclosure`, and paragraphs are bound with `{{ }}`. | §3 already says the actions a notice refers to sit inside it, "so the explanation and the way out are one object" — so the caution and its link are one object rather than a link bolted beside it. Keeping the prose itself markup-free is deliberate: a guide table that could carry markup could carry anything, and the table is edited far more often than the component. | No — §4 amended with the byline/notice/related-tools pattern |
| 2026-08-24 | **Two claims I wrote into the design contract were wrong, and the browser is what said so.** §3's new footer paragraph named the wrap point ("breaks after *Verify a signed PDF*") and promised no stranded separator. At a true 390 px it breaks after *E-sign disclosure* — the earlier reading came from a 500 px window, which is what macOS clamps Chrome to — and a middot does end the wrapped line. | Both corrected in the contract rather than in prose that describes them away. The break point is now specified as a *behaviour* (order preserved, no horizontal scroll at any width) with both measurements recorded as illustrations; the stranded separator is **accepted with its reasoning**, because the only layout that avoids it is a gap-based flex row with no middots, which would restyle the five-link footer the contract has pinned since 2026-08-06 — a bigger change than adding two links, and not one this phase was sanctioned to make. | **Yes — §3 Footer rewritten, twice** |
| 2026-08-24 | **The 404-status e2e assertion `test.skip`s on the dev server instead of asserting 200 and calling it a pass.** The e2e stack serves the frontend with `ng serve`, which answers every unknown path 200 with the SPA shell; nginx keeps the 404 via `error_page 404 /index.html`. | A test that quietly asserts the wrong thing on the only origin it ever runs against is worse than one that says what it cannot check. The spec asserts the *page* on any origin — NotFound renders, no guide chrome, which is what would break if the routes were ever made parameterised — and skips the *status* with the reason spelled out. It was then **run where it does hold**: real nginx, the Railway config, the built bundle, all seven phase-11 tests green including that one, `/guides/does-not-exist` → **404**. | No |
| 2026-08-24 | **The phone workspace's shell state lives in a third facade, `WorkspaceShellFacade` — not in the workspace component and not in `ViewerFacade`.** | The prompt offered exactly those two homes. The component is ruled out by the shape of the thing: the drawers *are* the seven panes' rails and the bar that opens them is drawn by the workspace, so opener and drawer are always in different components — `@Input`-threading a breakpoint through seven panes is a worse abstraction than a facade. `ViewerFacade` is ruled out because it is about the *document* — its versions, its outline, its errors — and none of this outlives a viewport change, let alone a document. A third, small facade keeps both of them honest, and `reset()` on pane destroy is what stops one mode's Save from being offered by the next. | **Yes — design contract §3 Phone workspace.** |
| 2026-08-24 | **A drawer is a bottom *sheet*, never a side drawer.** | A phone is held in one hand and the reachable third of the screen is the bottom. A side drawer on a 390 px screen is a full-width panel that has arrived from the wrong direction: same coverage, worse reach, and a horizontal slide that fights the page's own scroll axis. The sheet is capped at `62dvh`, sits on top of the bottom bar rather than over it, and is dismissed by the two gestures a sheet teaches — the scrim and the handle's own Close. | **Yes — §3.** |
| 2026-08-24 | **No mode opens its drawer by default, and the Annotate palette stays a drawer rather than becoming a compact bottom row.** | Two refusals, both of what the prompt suggested. Opening a panel on arrival answers a question nobody asked — the person navigated to a *document*, and every panel is one 44 px tap away. The palette is **seventeen** mutually exclusive tools and only a handful have a glyph on the §2 grid, so a row means either seventeen new icons — a bigger change than this one — or an unlabelled scroll of look-alikes; the same rail also carries colour, width, opacity, font size, the stamp picker and the custom-stamp upload, which are not a row at all; and a third fixed row at the bottom would leave a 390 × 844 screen with under half its height for the page. A labelled sheet you open beats a cryptic row you cannot avoid. | **Yes — §3, with both reasons written down.** |
| 2026-08-24 | **The workspace bar's six-button cluster is one `<ng-template>` rendered in one of two places, never two copies.** | Below `md` it belongs in the More sheet and above `md` on the bar, and the obvious implementation — render both, hide one — puts two `[data-test=download]` in the DOM at every width. A suite that has to say *which* Download it means is a suite that will one day mean the wrong one, and the four existing specs that click `[data-test=download]` would have gone strict-mode-red on a change that has nothing to do with them. `ngTemplateOutlet` costs one import and keeps the count at exactly one, which a unit case now asserts for all fifteen bar `data-test`s at both widths. | No |
| 2026-08-24 | **The pane's Undo/Redo/primary are *published* to the bar, and its own copies are hidden below `md` — moved, not duplicated.** | The bar cannot reach into six different undo stacks and the panes cannot reach the bar, so each pane pushes `{undo, redo, primary}` into the shell facade and the bar draws whatever is there. The page bar's own pair then gets `.ws-hoisted`, which is `display: contents` above `md` — so the desktop row is byte-identical — and `display: none` below it. Hiding rather than removing is deliberate: every existing `data-test` stays in the DOM, so no spec written for a desk breaks on a phone. Convert publishes **nothing**: it has three peer actions and no single primary, and an empty dock is a more honest answer than a disabled Save. | **Yes — §3.** |
| 2026-08-24 | **`data-test=ws-drawer` goes on the sheet's head, not on the rail, and the rail gets `data-ws-drawer` instead.** | One rail already carries a `data-test` of its own (`comments-sidebar`) and parity means keeping it, so a host binding would have overwritten it at exactly one of the nine drawers — the sort of thing that passes review and fails once. The head is also the more honest assertion: it exists only while the rail *is* a drawer, so its presence is the claim rather than a proxy for it. | **Yes — §3's data-test list, with the reason.** |
| 2026-08-24 | **Tapping the mode already selected re-selects it; it does not toggle back to View.** | That is what the desktop `.seg` does, and it is a useful accelerator with a pointer. On a phone the same tap is how a person checks where they are, and a tap that silently throws away the mode is a trap. `aria-pressed` already says which mode is on, and View is the first button in the row — the way out is visible rather than hidden in a gesture. | **Yes — §3.** |
| 2026-08-24 | **The unit runner gets a 15 s per-test budget, in `frontend/vitest.config.ts` through the builder's own `runnerConfig` hook.** | The gate went red twice on `workspace-drew.spec.ts`'s first case with `Test timed out in 5000ms` — a spec this branch never touches, and the sixth recorded appearance of the false red `infra/test.sh` already carries a long comment about. What is new is that it stopped being random: measured in the project's own container, back to back, that case takes **468 ms** when its file runs alone, **1 197 ms** in the whole suite before these specs, **3 110 ms** after them (58 → 62 files), and over 5 000 alongside the rest of the gate. Almost all of it is Angular compiling the 500-line workspace template and seven pane components — a per-*worker* cost that lands inside the **first** `it()` of the file — so the default budget measures how busy the box is rather than what the code does. Four new spec files pushed a machine already at the edge over it, which makes the budget mine to fix rather than the environment's to be blamed. 15 s is 3× the default and ~5× the worst honest measurement, so a real hang still fails — just later; larger would stop being a budget. The config was proven to be *read* by setting it to 1 ms and watching all four cases fail with `Test timed out in 1ms`, because a green run proves nothing about a file the runner might be ignoring. | No |
| 2026-08-24 | **The stacked-rail rules were deleted, not kept behind another breakpoint.** | The 2026-08-20 rescue capped each rail at `34vh` above and below the page. Keeping it for some narrower band would leave two phone layouts to reason about and one of them untested — and the rescue's own problem (the palette owning the top third at 390) is worse the narrower the screen gets, not better. Nothing may be left behind that no width can reach, so the rules are gone and §3's stacking paragraph with them. | **Yes — §3, with what it replaced recorded.** |

## Blockers

**2026-08-02 — `mypy` and `angular-eslint`: closed, and how.** *(Superseded. The
original entry is preserved below the line because the reasoning it records —
why a checker nobody gates on grows to 98 findings — is the reason this was
finally worth doing.)*

Both are now clean and both run in `infra/test.sh`. mypy went 98 → 72 with the
django-stubs plugin, then 72 → 0; three of the seventy-two were latent bugs and
are fixed, and seven `type: ignore`s mark where the stub is wrong and the
runtime was verified right. `angular-eslint` — note the name; `@angular/eslint`,
which this file named for ten phases, has never existed — is installed, clean,
and found twelve unlabelled form controls that axe structurally could not see
because they live behind panel tabs the scan never opens. TypeScript `strict` is
on; both projects already compiled clean under it.

Type-aware linting is deliberately still open, with its own queue row.

---

*Original entry, 2026-08-02, before the debt pass:*

> The structural half is done: `django-stubs` and `djangorestframework-stubs`
> are installed and the plugin is configured, which is what the queue item from
> Phase 0 actually named. That took the count from 98 to 72, and the ones it
> removed were mypy not knowing what a queryset is — noise that was hiding the
> real findings. The other 69 are typing hygiene spread across nineteen files.
> Working through them is a mechanical sweep with real regression risk, taken
> at the end of a phase that has already changed migrations, deletion semantics
> and the ad layer, and the value is modest against that. So: recorded rather
> than rushed.

## Session log — Phase 2B (2026-08-01)

**➡ Handoff — Phase 3 is next.** Start at [phase-03-annotations.md](phase-03-annotations.md); it builds the shared overlay layer (`PageOverlayComponent`, §7) reused by phases 4/5/7/8, and is unchanged by 2B except that annotation authorship must now use the principal model. `prompt-2-phases-03-07.md`'s precondition banner can be updated to **"Phases 0–2B are ✅"**.

What Phase 3 must know about the 2B refactor:
- **Ownership goes through `apps/core/principals.py` and nowhere else.** New querysets use `owned_by(qs, principal)`, new rows use `owner_kwargs(...)` / `job_owner_kwargs(...)`, worker code uses `principal_of(job)`. A grep test fails the build on `request.user`, `job.user`, `owner=` or `context["request"].user` outside that module — including in new code.
- **New endpoints are guest-accessible by default.** `IsPrincipal` is the project default permission; an account-only endpoint must say so with `IsAccount` **and** carry a written reason in §21.3.
- **New limits belong in `settings.TIERS` and resolve through `core.limits.for_principal()`.** Nothing hardcodes a limit at a call site. Note that `override_settings(MAX_UPLOAD_MB=…)` no longer changes behaviour — override `TIERS` (helper: `test_ingest.tiers_with`).
- **New tool pages append to `TOOL_PAGES` in `frontend/src/app/core/tool-pages.ts`.** Routes, prerendering and `sitemap.xml` are all generated from that table, and tests fail if they drift. Phase 3's slug is `/annotate-pdf`.
- **Anything metered goes in `METERED_OPS`, not the `heavy` queue.** They are deliberately different sets.
- Two Phase-2B acceptance criteria are **carried into Phase 8** (guest `self_sign`, guest sign-request → `account_required`) because those features do not exist yet. They are a GATE on Phase 8 sign-off.

**Preflight.** Cleared the planning session's leftovers: stale `.git/index.lock` (0 bytes, 2026-07-31 19:54; the only `git` process running was the long-lived `fsmonitor--daemon`, not a writer) and the empty `development-plans/_to_delete/`. All uncommitted paths confirmed under `development-plans/`. Toolchain: git 2.50.1, Docker 29.4.1, `gh` authenticated.

**Shipped.** Backend: `core.GuestSession` (256-bit token stored hashed, 24 h sliding TTL capped at 72 h); `Document`/`Job`/`UsageCounter` repointed to a principal with an exactly-one-of DB `CheckConstraint`; `User.plan`; `apps/core/principals.py` as the sole ownership expression; `core/limits.py` with `for_principal()` and `METERED_OPS`; `PrincipalAuthentication` + `IsPrincipal`/`IsAccount` + `account_required`/`guest_expired`/`captcha_required`; the worker layer routed through `principal_of(job)`; storage accounting moved off the `users` table; **`MAX_PAGES` enforced for the first time**; tier-resolved concurrency; `POST /api/guest/session/` and `POST /api/guest/claim/` plus inline claim on register/login; guest throttles keyed on token *and* salted IP; a Turnstile adapter gated on `METERED_OPS` only; hourly `guest_purge`. Frontend: `GuestFacade`, the interceptor credential branch, guest-aware 401/410, `accountGuard` in place of the app-wide guard, the viewer's `httpHeaders` bypass, session/claim/upgrade banners, seven public prerendered tool pages, and a generated `sitemap.xml`/`robots.txt`.

**Bugs this phase found and fixed (each caught by a test, not by review):**
1. **Parallel first-writes minted two sessions** — a guest dropping two files on `/merge-pdf` got them in different sessions, so the merge saw one. Fixed with `GuestFacade.ensureSession()`. Caught by e2e.
2. **Claim pre-flight measured the wrong number** — current-version sizes rather than the session's storage counter, so a session with edit history could pass pre-flight and still overflow the account. Caught by a failing claim test.
3. **The grep gate had a fourth hole** — `self.context["request"].user` matched none of the three named patterns once string literals were blanked out; and the allowlist already had 5 stale exemptions on its first run.
4. **Throttle counters were per-process** — no `CACHES` was configured, so DRF's throttles (and the new metered windows) used LocMem, meaning each API worker enforced a private limit.
5. **`TokenService` threw during prerendering** — unguarded `localStorage` access, invisible until SSR was switched on.

**Self-review pass (Step 8) — two further findings, both fixed on the branch:**
6. **`owned_by()` raised `FieldError` on account-only models.** `Folder` has no `guest_session` column, so `owned_by(Folder.objects.all(), guest)` was a 500 — reachable by a guest simply passing `folder=<id>` on upload. Fixed at the choke point (`accepts_guests()` → `qs.none()`), so it now protects every account-only model including the Phase-8 esign ones. `test_principals.py::test_owned_by_returns_nothing_for_a_guest_on_an_account_only_model`, `::test_guest_uploading_into_a_folder_gets_404_not_500`, `::test_owns_is_false_for_a_guest_against_an_account_only_model`.
7. **Operations minted a session *before* the ownership check.** `POST …/operations/`, `…/revert/` and `/api/operations/` all passed `write=True`, so anyone POSTing at random document ids created a `GuestSession` row per probe and then got a 404 anyway. Every one of those endpoints targets a document the caller must already own, so none of them needs to mint — only upload (and the explicit endpoint) does. `test_guest.py::test_probing_document_ids_never_mints_a_session`.

Final numbers after the review fixes: backend **232 passed**, frontend **40**, e2e **7**; `apps` coverage **91%**; schema still 0 warnings/0 errors.

**Baseline.** First full run was red on **e2e `phase-2.spec.ts`** — `[data-test=confirm-ok]` never appeared after `op-delete`. Re-ran the spec alone (green) and the whole suite again (3/3 green), so it is a **flake, not a broken base**: the spec clicks `organize-page` `nth(0)` immediately after the rotate toast, and the `viewer.reload()` → `pages.clear()` effect can wipe that selection mid-click, in which case `op-delete` shows "Select one or more pages first" instead of the confirm dialog. Slower on the session's first run because `ng serve` compiles cold. Not fixed here — it belongs to Phase 2 and this phase does not own it; logged in the Human review queue instead.

## Session log — Phase 3 (2026-08-01)

**Shipped.** Backend: `pdf_engine/engine/annotations.py` (extract / apply-ops / flatten) and `engine/text.py::page_words`; `annotate_batch` + `flatten` in the registry with per-type JSON Schemas; `GET …/annotations/` and `GET …/text-words/`; `core/assets.py` + `POST /api/uploads/image/` for ephemeral image assets; `geometry.py` gains the point pair. Frontend: `shared/page-overlay/` — the generic overlay primitive phases 4/5/7/8 will reuse — plus `AnnotationsFacade`, annotate mode (palette, page navigation, zoom, comments sidebar, save/autosave/flatten), the `/annotate-pdf` tool page, and crop migrated onto the overlay.

**Bugs this phase found, each caught by a test rather than by review:**
1. **Two MuPDF segfaults, not exceptions.** Creating/deleting a page invalidates every live `Page`/`Annot` handle (the image-stamp path did exactly that); and `doc[i].annots()` lets the temporary `Page` be collected while its annots are still in use, because `annot.parent` is a weak reference. Both are commented at the call site.
2. **A `/Text` note yields two `/Annots` entries** — itself and its `/Popup`. The first version of the golden assertion counted raw entries and failed; counting non-Popup entries is what the criterion actually means.
3. **A signed-in user's unsaved marks were attributed to "Guest."** The author is stamped server-side, so a local draft has none and the fallback was wrong for accounts. Found by reading the e2e failure snapshot.
4. **The overlay raster was capped at the 1200 px thumbnail limit**, making precise placement guesswork on HiDPI. Cap raised to 2000 and the client request clamped to it.
5. **An off-by-one in the ink point cap** — thinning then appending the endpoint produced 2001 points against a stated cap of 2000. Replacing the last kept sample fixes it and keeps the pen-up point.
6. **The e2e note step was a genuine test-synchronisation bug, not app breakage**: the palette sets the tool, which reaches the overlay as an input, and the click fired first. The spec now waits for the observable consequence (the text layer is gone). The same debugging pass showed the default 720 px viewport cannot reach the lower half of a rendered page — earlier drags only worked because pointer capture retargets off-screen events.

**Not automated (recorded, not hidden):** the 30 s autosave timer and the native `beforeunload` prompt. Playwright cannot assert a native unload dialog, and a 30 s wall-clock wait is a flake generator; the composer both paths call is unit-tested instead.

**Final numbers:** backend **314**, frontend **62**, e2e **10**; `apps` coverage **91%**, `pdf_engine` **91%**; schema 0 warnings / 0 errors.

**➡ Handoff — Phase 4 is next.** `shared/page-overlay/` is the layer to build on: map your model onto `OverlayItem`, consume `OverlayDraft`, and never fork it. `GET …/text-words/` already exists and phase-04's `text-blocks` read model belongs beside it in `engine/text.py`. `POST /api/uploads/image/` already provides the image refs `add_image`/`replace_image`/image watermarks need — resolve them worker-side with `core.assets.load_images(principal_of(job), refs)`.

## Session log — Phase 4 (2026-08-02)

**Shipped.** Backend: `pdf_engine/engine/content.py` with 17 operations (`edit_text`, `add_text`, `whiteout`, `find_replace`, `add_image`/`replace_image`/`delete_image`, `add_link`/`edit_link`/`delete_link`, `header_footer`, `page_numbers`, `bates`, `watermark`, `overlay_pdf`, `set_metadata`, `set_bookmarks`), the `text-blocks`/`images`/`links` read models, and `pdf_engine/colors.py` shared with Phase 3. Frontend: `EditFacade` and Edit mode (Edit / Stamps / Info tabs) built on the Phase-3 overlay unchanged, plus three tool pages.

**Found by tests, not review:**
1. **The coordinate family is inconsistent** — `insert_htmlbox` applies page rotation, its siblings do not. Measured across all five writers before committing to a rule.
2. **`search_for` cannot do case-sensitive search at all**, which the phase doc assumes it can.
3. **Ligatures break search on edited text** — and, it turns out, on any LaTeX/InDesign PDF the product has ever opened.
4. **A find & replace dry run was minting a version**, putting "Replaced 0 matches" in the history every time somebody searched. It now returns a report and touches nothing.
5. **The two halves of find & replace answered different result shapes**; unified so a client cannot read the wrong key.
6. **An SVG shape with `fill="none"` is only clickable on its 1px border** — text blocks were effectively unselectable. Fixed in the shared overlay, so Phase 3 inherits it.
7. **The overlay's loading placeholder intercepted pointer events.**

**Final numbers:** backend **438**, frontend **82**, e2e **17**; `apps` coverage **92%**, `pdf_engine` **92%**; schema 0 warnings / 0 errors.

**Self-review (Step 3.7) — eleven further defects, all fixed on the branch.** The regression lens came back clean; the other three found them. The two that mattered most were both about rotated pages: `search_for` answers in *unrotated* space (the code asserted the opposite), which broke find & replace, `match_case`, the review-list context and the replacement's style on any rotated page — **and had been silently breaking Phase 1's find bar since it shipped**; and every text edit on a landscape-rotated page failed with `text_overflow` telling the user their text was too long. Also: adjacent occurrences were silently deleted ("banana" + "na" reported one match and dropped one), a same-length-but-wider replacement ("cat"→"CAT") aborted the whole job, `get_image_rects` decoded every embedded image in the API process (1.1 MB PDF → 1.7 GB) and multiplied duplicate placements N², all three read models returned 500 for ordinary print PDFs, `watermark.rotation` was silently snapped to 90° steps so the spec'd −45° rendered vertical, `fits_at_size` never reached the client, and staged edits were keyed by a per-page block number so editing page 2 destroyed page 1's edit.

**➡ Handoff — Phase 5 is next.** The overlay takes field rectangles the same way it took annotations and text blocks: map to `OverlayItem`, consume `OverlayDraft`. `flatten` already supports `what=form` and is tested (`test_flatten_form_leaves_annotations_alone`). `core.assets` provides image refs if the form builder needs them. Note for Phase 6: flipping the scanned-page gate on is a single input — `<app-edit [ocrAvailable]="true">` — plus wiring `ocrRequested` to the OCR dialog.

## Session log — Phase 5 (2026-08-02)

**Shipped.** Backend: `pdf_engine/engine/forms.py` (read model, fill, six-type field builder, import/export, flatten via the existing `flatten what=form`), four operations (`fill_form`, `edit_form_fields_batch`, `import_form_data`, plus `flatten`), and the `…/form/` + `…/form/export/` endpoints. Frontend: `FormsFacade` and Forms mode — Fill (PDF.js widgets mirrored through `[(formData)]`, plus a field panel, export/import and flatten-with-confirm) and Form builder (draw a box on the Phase-3 overlay, property panel, one batch job) — and the `/fill-pdf-form` tool page.

**Four PyMuPDF limits, all measured rather than assumed:** radio widgets are unusable (built at the pikepdf level instead); an empty `/V` is not written by `update()`; `/AcroForm` may be inline, so XFA detection needs the path lookup; and PyMuPDF *can* create `/Sig` fields, contradicting the phase doc — pyHanko is still used, for Phase 8's sake. Each is in the Decisions log with its measurement.

**Two bugs found by the E2E run that had nothing to do with forms:**
1. **The PDF viewer had no credential.** `principal()` is a computed over a non-signal getter, so it cached "logged out" from the login page and never recovered: every viewer fetch went out anonymous and 404'd, in view mode as well as fill mode. `TokenService` now holds the token in a signal.
2. **A job that failed before its handler stayed `running` for ever.** `get_op` sat outside the try, so a worker with a stale registry left the client polling a job that would never finish.

**Self-review (Step 3.7) — the four lenses found eighteen further defects, all fixed on the branch.** The correctness lens was the sharpest: a radio option that is not already a bare PDF name could never be selected — including `Option 1`, which is the builder's *own default text* — because the option is stored as a PDF name, escaped on write and handed back raw; any field placed on two pages (a signature line repeated on every page of a contract, the commonest AcroForm shape there is) was misread as a radio group and had its value erased; radio kids stayed registered as top-level fields while a deleted group left a dangling parent, so an update produced two fields with the same name; and an export→import round trip wrote an empty `/V` onto every `/Sig` field, which makes pyHanko refuse to sign it. The security lens confirmed ownership, filenames, PDF-name injection and data remanence were clean, and found the two unbounded params in the schema file, a 900 KB error message, and CSV formula injection in the export. The regression lens caught that the token fix had turned every browser tab into a private session. The conformance lens found phase-05's named appearance-stream check missing, `align` write-only, and the pyHanko flip living outside `geometry.py`.

**Final numbers:** backend **534**, frontend **114**, e2e **21**; `apps` coverage **92%**, `pdf_engine` **93%**; schema 0 warnings / 0 errors.

**➡ Handoff — Phase 6 is next.** The scanned-page gate is one input away from live: `<app-edit [ocrAvailable]="true">` plus wiring `ocrRequested`. `METERED_OPS` already names ocr/convert_from/convert_to/compare and `core.limits` enforces the hourly window, so the new ops need no new metering machinery. Forms leaves a signature-field placeholder behind for Phase 8 (`_add_signature_field` writes a real `/Sig` field through pyHanko, which is what `append_signature_field` will sign into).

## Session log — Phase 6 (2026-08-02)

**Shipped.** Backend: `engine/ocr.py` (OCRmyPDF in-process, five language packs, failure taxonomy), `engine/convert.py` (Gotenberg for office/HTML/URL, PyMuPDF for images in and images/text/HTML out, pdf2docx, pymupdf4llm, PDF/A through Ghostscript), `engine/compare.py` (word-level text diff + clustered visual diff), `core/urlguard.py`, the `export` result kind with `GET /api/jobs/{id}/download/`, and `POST /api/uploads/source/`. Frontend: `ConvertFacade` + `CompareFacade`, Convert and Compare modes, the unified dashboard import (files and addresses), and eight tool pages in three shapes.

**The Phase-4 gate is now live.** Its CTA was shipped disabled with a "coming with OCR tool" tooltip; the e2e now walks the whole arc — the editor refuses a scan, the CTA opens the OCR panel, and afterwards the editor opens on the same document and the find bar locates a word only OCR could have added.

**Found by tests, not review:**
1. **An image document cannot be drawn onto a PDF page** — `show_pdf_page` refuses anything that is not a PDF, so the obvious route to image-import raises "is no PDF". Converting first also made multi-frame TIFFs work for free.
2. **A file that will not open at all** reached PyMuPDF's own error type instead of ours, so "not a PDF" surfaced as a crash rather than a message.
3. **`page.set_cropbox` takes fitz-space coordinates, not PDF ones** — measured while pinning the signature-box conversion from Phase 5.

**Self-review (Step 3.7) — the four lenses found twenty further defects, all fixed on the branch.** Two were confirmed by exploitation rather than by reading. **The deny-list let `http://redis.:6379/` through**: a trailing dot is a valid FQDN spelling that Docker DNS resolves identically, and layer 2 is the *only* guard on HTML subresources — layer 1 never sees those — so an uploaded `<iframe src>` returned our SeaweedFS bucket listing and Mailpit's mailbox inside the converted PDF. **`convert_from` was the one route into the library with no page cap**: a 288 KB 2 000-frame TIFF became a 2 000-page document for a guest capped at 300. Beyond those: nothing enforced the export TTL the UI promises on two screens; conversion sources were charged to storage and never freed; OCR pages were never counted while `/api/config/` advertised the limit; a positive compare offset silently dropped the first N pages of B — the cover page the offset exists to align — and then reported "identical"; compare's text rects were unrotated coordinates divided by display dimensions, so on a rotated page the highlight landed most of a page away and the two panes disagreed; `_probe` accepted a PNG as a PDF; a URL's credentials became the document title; both facades left `busy` stuck on any HTTP error, which for metered operations meant a guest's sixth export killed the panel until a page reload; and `/jpg-to-pdf` produced N separate documents where its own copy promised one.

**Final numbers:** backend **681**, frontend **133**, e2e **29**; `apps` coverage **92%**, `pdf_engine` **92%**; schema 0 warnings / 0 errors.

**➡ Handoff — Phase 7 is next.** Redaction reuses the Phase-3 overlay for its boxes exactly as forms did, and `engine/content.py::whiteout` is the *wrong* primitive to build on (it hides pixels and leaves the text — the distinction the Phase-4 UI already explains to users). `apps/core/urlguard.py` is the pattern for phase-7's own network-facing checks if any appear. Note for Phase 8: `_add_signature_field` already writes a real `/Sig` field through pyHanko, and `geometry.page_rect_to_pdf_native` is the conversion its visible-signature boxes need.

## Session log — Phase 7 (2026-08-02)

**Shipped.** Backend: `engine/security.py` (AES-256/R6 through pikepdf, graded print/modify permissions mapped onto the flags the spec actually has, sanitize with a per-item report) and `engine/redact.py` (five presets, regex and plain search over *reconstructed line text* so a phone number written across four words is found, area redaction, and a verification pass that re-reads the result); `Job.SENSITIVE_PARAMS` with redaction on the way out and erasure on every terminal state; `document_password` as a request-level field; a per-document guessing throttle. Frontend: `DocumentPasswords` (memory only) attached centrally by `DocumentsService`, `SecurityFacade`, the three-tab Protect mode (password + permissions in plain language, two-step redaction with a review list drawn on the Phase-3 overlay, sanitize checklist), a type-the-title confirm, and the `/protect-pdf` `/unlock-pdf` `/redact-pdf` pages.

**The distinction this phase exists to make good on.** Phase 4's whiteout hides content and says so; redaction deletes it. `apply_redactions(images=PDF_REDACT_IMAGE_PIXELS)` destroys the pixels under the box as well as the glyphs, and the test proves it by rendering the page and looking for the colour.

**Found by tests, not review:**
1. **The criterion's own raw-bytes grep was vacuous.** PyMuPDF writes text as hex strings inside deflated streams, so `b"dana.cohen" not in pdf_bytes` was true *before* redaction. `raw_text_bytes()` inflates and decodes; every such test now first asserts the fixture contains the string.
2. **Sanitize left the content in the file.** Removing a comment from `/Annots` unlinks it — qpdf keeps the orphan, and a note's `/Popup` holds a reference back to it. Fixed with a `garbage=4` pass and by removing the popup with its parent; the test greps the bytes.
3. **A thumbnail of a locked document was a 500**, one per page in the strip. Now a 423 the client can act on.
4. **Editing an unlocked document silently unprotected it** — found by the e2e, which unlocked, rotated a page, and then could not remove a password that was no longer there. The behaviour is unavoidable (see the Decisions log); what was wrong was that nothing said so. Now the banner warns first and the version label records it.
5. **The password prompt fired at the user who had just chosen the password** — it keyed off a second copy of the state instead of the session store.

**Self-review (Step 3.7) — the four lenses found sixteen further defects, all fixed on the branch.** The three that would have hurt somebody:

1. **"Remove hidden layers" *revealed* them.** Dropping `/OCProperties` is what the phase text says ("set visible state + drop OC") and it is the opposite of what the checkbox promises: with the layer machinery gone, the content the user could not see renders on the page — and the report counted it as removed. The engine now cuts the off layers' marked-content sections out of the page streams first, verified against a fixture that gained a genuinely hidden layer for the purpose.
2. **Unticking every match in the review list redacted every match.** `only: []` read as "no filter" rather than "none of these" — the exact inversion of the user's instruction, on the one operation that cannot be undone.
3. **The verification pass called a correct redaction a failure.** It re-ran every pattern over every page, so the match the user deliberately kept came back as "still findable" — which is the phase's own flow (find three, untick one, apply two) and would have shown a red error on every use of the review list.

The rest, in one line each: the session password reached four engines that splat `params` and crashed them; the clean-copy path — the *default* — dropped the verification report entirely; copies taken from an unlocked document lost their protection with no notice; reverting to an encrypted version left the document flagged unprotected; the password prompt reopened after the password was removed and never closed; a `scope` naming no real page silently meant *every* page; the phone preset matched any eight digits and ate three lines off the fixture's own "must survive" list; a card number preceded by a row number was missed entirely; an IBAN printed in groups of four was missed; a user regex could pin both heavy workers until the 900 s kill (`(a+)+` — now under a 20 s alarm); the stall reaper flipped jobs terminal with a queryset `.update()`, leaving password material in the database for ever; a redaction preview kept the secrets it found on the job row indefinitely (now swept after an hour); area ids were reused after a deletion, so removing one area removed another; and the redaction draft survived a navigation to a *different* document.

**The e2e suite was fighting the auth throttle**, not the app: 10 registrations a minute per IP is a production number, and the suite is one IP. Relaxed in dev settings only.

**Final numbers:** backend **800**, frontend **153**, e2e **38**; `apps` coverage **91%**, `pdf_engine` **92%**; schema 0 warnings / 0 errors.

**➡ Handoff — Phase 8 is next.** It carries the **GATE** from Phase 2B: guest `self_sign` via `signature_upload_ref`, and a guest sign-*request* refused with `account_required`. `_add_signature_field` (Phase 5) already writes a real `/Sig` field through pyHanko and `geometry.page_rect_to_pdf_native` is the conversion its visible-signature boxes need. Two Phase-7 pieces are directly reusable: `core/assets.py` for the uploaded signature image, and `SEC.open_with_password` — a signing op on a protected document goes through the same central unlock, with the same "protection comes off" consequence, which the signing UI will have to say too.

## Session log — Phase 8 (2026-08-02)

**Shipped.** Backend: `apps/esign` — five models with `AuditEvent` as a hash chain, the routing engine, the public ceremony, `finalize_sign_request` (burn → stamp → PAdES seal → certificate → notify), reminders and expiry on beat, and `/api/verify/`. Engine: `signatures.py` (draw/type/upload → trimmed transparent PNG, `self_sign` with flattening, the envelope footer) and `seal.py` (pyHanko sign + validate). Frontend: the signature pad, the self-sign tab, the four-step request builder, the mobile-first ceremony, the owner's request detail with the audit trail, `/verify`, and the `/sign-pdf` tool page.

**The Phase-2B gate is cleared**, and its tripwire test did its job: `test_no_guest_reachable_route_accepts_a_recipient_address` was written in 2B to *fail* the moment sign-request routes existed, with the instruction "assert it declares IsAccount before shipping". It fired on the first full run of this phase, and now resolves every such route to its view and checks the permission class.

**Found by tests, not review:**
1. **`fitz.Pixmap(src, w, h, clip)` segfaults** — the obvious way to crop a drawn signature to its ink. The crop is built from raw samples instead; a crash inside the worker is not a failure mode worth flirting with.
2. **A POST per keystroke** in the ceremony, which the e2e found by hitting the public throttle. It was also writing one audit event per letter typed.
3. **The migration had never been applied to the dev database** — the API 500'd on the first real request. Caught by the e2e, not by the unit suites, which build their own schema.

**Self-review (Step 3.7) — the four lenses found twenty-six further defects, all fixed on the branch.** The five that mattered most:

1. **`/verify` called a forged contract intact.** `status.intact` covers only the signed byte range, and a PDF incremental update appends a revision, leaves that range alone and changes what the document says. pyHanko calls that `ENTIRE_REVISION`; the verifier called it "unchanged since". Whole-file coverage is now required before the word intact is used.
2. **The seal was not PAdES**, while the report written into the audit chain called it "B-B". pyHanko's default subfilter is the legacy Adobe profile — a level name recorded as *evidence* has to describe the file that was actually produced.
3. **A typed signature was an opaque black rectangle.** The paper-removal pass ran over a transparent render, whose unpainted background is (0,0,0,0) — grey value 0, i.e. "as dark as ink". Every person who typed their name would have stamped a black box on the contract.
4. **Two concurrent audit writes forked the chain**, permanently: it is append-only, so `verify_chain` would have reported broken for ever on a request where nothing wrong had happened. Two taps on Download were enough.
5. **The ceremony had no viewer.** The phase asks for the document with the field widgets positioned on it; the first cut shipped a list of cards and a link out to the PDF. A signer who cannot see where their mark lands is signing blind.

The rest, in one line each: a signature on a rotated page landed off the edge and then lay on its side; `insert_textbox` silently dropped dates and ticks that did not fit; an uploaded photograph kept its paper; the finalize wrote its seal to storage *before* claiming the status, and ran on a queue that kills it at 60 s; permanently deleting a document sent for signature emptied its storage and then 500'd; the completion email's download links pointed at a route that does not exist; a forwarded link cost a later signer their "it's your turn" email; clearing a text field never reached the server; the last signer was told the others had not signed; the ceremony's typed-signature preview carried the browser's credential and fired per keystroke; a guest was shown "create a free account" for merely opening the Sign tab; the `/verify` signer roster was readable from the envelope code alone; the chain was self-consistent rather than keyed; `POST /api/signatures/` decoded an uncapped image in the API process; two public endpoints declared a throttle scope that did nothing; a saved signature's PNG outlived its row; `int("soon")` was a 500; and the finalize wrote a version outside the document lock.

**Final numbers:** backend **870**, frontend **163**, e2e **43**; `apps` coverage **91%**, `pdf_engine` **92%**; schema 0 warnings / 0 errors.

**➡ Handoff — Phase 9 is next.** Two things it must know. First, **§8C is ad-free**: `/verify` and `/s/:token` are trust surfaces, and phase-08 states that normatively — whatever ad component Phase 9 adds must not mount on those routes, and the same goes for the consent screen. Second, Phase 9's abuse controls meet an existing tripwire: `apps/core/captcha.py` keys on `METERED_OPS`, which is deliberately *not* the heavy queue, and `test_limits.py` asserts the two sets stay disjoint. The guest-abuse suite (`test_guest_abuse.py`) is the file to extend rather than replace.

## Session log — Phase 9 (2026-08-02)

**Branch** `feat/phase-09-ads-abuse` → PR #9. Three commits: the phase itself, the review-finding fixes, and the test updates that follow from them.

**What the phase built.** The ad layer behind one component and one flag (`ADS_ENABLED=false` is the shipped default, and with it the product loads no third-party code at all); consent wiring with Consent Mode defaults and a plain banner the certified CMP will replace; the legal pages, the About page and a shared footer; and the account-side abuse controls — email verification before sending, the suppression list and `List-Unsubscribe`, the report-abuse endpoint with its three-distinct-reports pause, the throttle matrix, Django admin with ban and soft-delete, the usage panel, and the storage-hygiene report.

**What the reviews found, and why it is worth recording.** Four lenses ran against the finished diff: security/privacy, correctness/regression, plan conformance, and a general read. Between them they returned twenty-three substantiated findings, two reproduced live against the running stack. The pattern is one worth naming: **the code worked and the promises around it did not.** The unsubscribe header was well-formed and pointed somewhere that could not honour it. The suppression list worked and the audit trail claimed the mail had gone anyway. The upload cap worked and was spent by page loads. The consent banner worked and would not have appeared for the visitors it exists for. Every one of those passes a test that asks "does the code do what it says"; none passes a test that asks "is the sentence we published true".

**What that changed about the tests.** The new ones assert the promise rather than the mechanism: that a 429 on uploads still leaves the library readable, that the prerendered privacy page and the beat sweeper quote the same number, that a thank-you is not rendered as an error, that the usage panel is reporting a real OCR run rather than a row of zeroes.

**Deviations from the plan, all recorded above.** Declining consent yields no ads rather than non-personalized ones. The signup Turnstile sits on register and on completing a signature, not on opening a signing link. Account deletion stays a request-by-email path and the legal copy was corrected to say so, rather than growing a route this phase did not scope.

**One flake observed, not fixed here.** `phase-8.spec.ts::two signers in order` failed once during a full-suite run and passed on the next full run and in isolation; the stubbed-config race in `phase-9.spec.ts` (Playwright disposing a `route.fetch()` response mid-navigation) *was* fixed, by snapshotting `/api/config/` before installing the route. The phase-8 one is timing in another phase's spec and is left for its owner.

**Environment.** The macOS virtiofs duplicate-file artefact recurred (`* 2.*` files break ruff and `ng build`); the tracked `auth.interceptor 2.ts` orphan was finally removed, since it was inside `src/` and compiled. The frontend Vitest run OOM'd once and passed on re-run — the known Docker VM memory item.

## Session log — Phase 10 (2026-08-02)

**Branch** `feat/phase-10-hardening-release`. No new features, which is the
phase's own instruction — everything here is about what happens when the
product meets production, hostile input, or somebody who cannot use a mouse.

**What the hostile corpus found.** Four of the five fixtures confirmed the
stack was already robust: MuPDF refuses a self-referential page tree in 0.13 s,
a decompression bomb is never inflated by validation *or* by a sanitize pass,
a deep outline comes back bounded. The fifth found a real hole — a `.docx` is a
zip, and nothing checked what it claimed to unpack to before handing it to
LibreOffice. That is the value of writing the attack down rather than reasoning
about it.

**What axe found.** Not edge cases: `text-slate-400` body text at 2.85:1 across
most of the product, white-on-emerald buttons and toasts at 3.65:1, unlabelled
inputs on both auth screens, and a scrollable disclosure box a keyboard could
not reach — on the screen where somebody agrees to sign electronically. The
keyboard-only ceremony pass is the assertion that matters, and it is written to
fail saying "this control is unreachable" rather than printing a count.

**What the dependency audit found.** Eighteen advisories in Pillow's image
decoders, on a product whose job is decoding files somebody else uploaded.
Bumped, along with `requests` (which fetches URLs users supply).

**What deletion turned out to require.** The privacy pages promised account
deletion; implementing it exposed that `SignRequest.owner` was `CASCADE`, so
closing an account destroyed the counterparty's evidence of a contract they had
signed. The fix — `SET_NULL` plus a sender snapshot taken at send — is a schema
change made for a legal reason rather than a technical one, and it is recorded
as such.

**Where this phase stops.** Four criteria are `[~]` and one is an open GATE,
and all five need the same thing: a deployed host with a domain, credentials and
somebody's signature. `v1.0.0` is deliberately **not** tagged. The phase doc
says this phase ends amber unless the owner completes their items, and they have
not, so it does.

**What the four review lenses found, and why it is worth recording.** Twenty-six
substantiated findings across security, correctness, plan conformance and UX —
and the pattern is the same one Phase 9 had: *the code worked and the promises
around it did not.* Deletion retained every envelope while four separate places
in the copy said "envelopes other people have already signed", so a request
nobody signed kept both sides' addresses and IP addresses to prove nothing. The
export button was an `<a href>` to a bearer-token endpoint, so "Download my
data" navigated into a 403 on the DRF browsable API — a promise the privacy
page had just started making. The health probe read the cache *outside* the
try, so a Redis outage produced a 500 with no payload rather than "the database
is fine, keep serving". The admin allowlist read `X-Forwarded-For` on a path
nginx does not proxy, so one header opened it. The composite index bought a
wider index and no benefit, because a null test keeps the column in the sort
key — measured on 500k rows before and after. And the a11y suite had a test
named for the workspace that never opened it.

**And one that was not a promise but a hole.** The security lens built a
padded zip bomb — 3 MB on the wire, 302 MB declared, two megabytes of
incompressible padding to fix the ratio — uploaded it, and watched Gotenberg
climb to 1.4 GB and stay dead for twenty-five minutes, because nothing
restarts it. The guard was arithmetic on numbers the uploader chose. It now
streams every entry through the decompressor against a hard cap and trusts
nothing declared, and the service restarts itself. That one is worth
remembering as the shape of the mistake: *a check whose inputs the attacker
supplies is not a check.*

All of those are fixed. What most of them have in common is that each one
passes a test asking "does the code do what it says" and fails a test asking
"is the sentence we published true" — which is the test worth writing.

**Honest note on the run.** Two frontend specs timed out on the first full-gate
run immediately after `up.sh`, and passed standalone in 4.8 s a minute later —
the known memory constraint on this machine, queued rather than papered over.

## Session log — Retention & quota (2026-08-02)

**Branch** `feat/retention`. Two Human-review-queue rows, both of which turned
out to be asking for the wrong thing.

**What the record got wrong, and what fixed it.** The first draft of this
change said "five prerendered tool pages" promise an unpruned history. Three
say it outright (`/organize-pdf`, `/annotate-pdf`, `/redact-pdf`) and six imply
it — five was neither count, and a reader who checked would have found the
Decisions log unreliable on its first testable claim. It also said the quota
was missing from one write path and called that "the fourth door and it was the
open one". There were four existing checks, not three (`store_source` was
omitted), and **two** open doors, not one: an adversarial pass found that
`split` reaches `_create_document_from_bytes`, which had no check either, from
the same endpoint that now refuses `rotate_pages` — 2 000 blobs on one request,
and a split→merge cycle growing the account every round. Both corrected in
place; the second door is now closed and tested.

**What the review found that the implementation missed.** Revert reads
`versions()`, and paginating that list without a pager made every version past
the fiftieth unrevertible — the change fixed the *appearance* of a truncated
history while removing access to it, which is the exact promise it declined to
prune history in order to keep. `jobs_purge` refunded on the presence of a
storage key rather than on the delete actually freeing something, which
double-credits any row whose blob `exports_purge` removed before dying between
its two un-transacted steps. Both fixed.

**What the third lens proved.** The correctness pass did not argue about the
second door — it instrumented it. `enforce_storage` read
`principal.storage_bytes_used` off the in-memory instance, which `bump_storage`
never refreshes, so the loop check passed 1 999 times on iteration 1's reading.
The fix reads the row; the batch is also measured whole, because a refusal that
lands on item *k* leaves the first *k-1* documents behind. Both are covered by
tests that fail without the fix — verified by reverting it.

**What was left open on purpose.** `_save_export` still charges without
refusing: an export is how somebody over quota gets their work out, and it
deletes itself in 24 h. Written down rather than closed, because "all the doors
are shut" is the sentence that stops the next reader looking.

**Record audit, 2026-08-03.** PROGRESS read against the code rather than
against itself, and three rows were wrong. The Phase-10 DoD still claimed 971
backend tests (988). The Phase-0 `mypy`/`eslint` row was still open although the
debt pass had closed both — mypy reports no issues across 166 files and `ng lint`
passes, and the row had also named a package (`@angular/eslint`) that has never
existed. And the orphaned `auth.interceptor 2.ts` row described a file that had
already been deleted in Phase 9 on the same day the row was filed; it was written
from a stale working tree instead of from `git ls-files`. Everything else
sampled held: every artefact a resolved row claims exists on disk, `check
--deploy` yields exactly the two warnings recorded as deliberate, and Phase 4's
"scanned gate awaits P6" note is satisfied — `onOcrRequested()` hands into the
convert panel.

**Verification.** Fresh stack, `reset.sh --yes && up.sh && test.sh --pg --e2e`:
988 backend + 178 frontend unit + 58 e2e + 8 query-plan tests green; coverage
`apps` 90.81 % (gate 85) and `pdf_engine` 91.88 % (gate 90); `spectacular
--fail-on-warn` exit 0; `manage.py check` no issues; ruff and mypy clean.

## Session log — QA findings (2026-08-04)

**Branch** `main`. Scope is `docs/reviews/IMPLEMENT-FINDINGS-PROMPT.md`, verbatim
and in order: three ship-blockers, seven mediums, fourteen polish items, one
commit per finding id. No features.

**The three ship-blockers, and what each actually was.**

*H1 was a config value, not a bug.* `client_ip` and DRF's `get_ident` were both
correct; `NUM_PROXIES=1` on a two-proxy deployment was not. One hop too few and
every request resolves to the TLS terminator's constant address, which collapses
`auth`, `verify`, `verify_hour`, `public_sign`, `image_upload`, `client_error`
and the guest-IP leg into a single global bucket — one caller can lock the whole
site out of login. The code is unchanged; the invariant is now written in all
three places an operator looks and pinned by
`apps/core/tests/test_client_ip_topology.py`, which also asserts the *failure*
so a future edit has to argue with it.

*H2 was a check the storage quota was standing in for and could not.* Duplicated
pages share their content xrefs, so `duplicate_pages` multiplies the page count
for almost no bytes. `test_version_page_cap.py` fails four ways without the fix,
verified by reverting it.

*H3 was measured, not reasoned about.* Under a 1 GiB `RLIMIT_AS` in the api
container, `fitz.Pixmap` on the 1.5 MB 40000×40000 fixture asks for
1 600 040 000 bytes; `header_dimensions` answers from the IHDR in six
microseconds. The finding also named a second door — the public ceremony's
field-fill had no byte cap at all — and both paths now share one constant.

**What the findings did not know, and the work found.** The Gotenberg deny-list
literal lives in **four** places, not the three M5 names: the fourth is
`infra/.env.example`, and *that* copy is the one in force, because the
environment wins over both the settings default and compose's `${VAR:-…}`
fallback. It had already drifted. So M5 is two fixes: the four missing ranges,
and a drift guard — a pytest asserting the running value has not fallen behind
the code, plus a check in `infra/test.sh` comparing the infra copies to each
other. The latter is in `test.sh` rather than in pytest because the api
container mounts `backend/` and nothing else; a test that skips is not a test.

**Where a test was allowed to change.** M3 contradicts two existing assertions
by design. `test_worker_limits.py::test_the_sweep_and_the_task_say_the_same_thing`
and `test_jobs.py::test_the_stall_reaper_drops_password_material` both aged their
fixture on `created_at`, which is the behaviour the finding calls wrong: a job
that merely waited behind a backlog was being told it "stopped responding". Both
now set `started_at`, and what they were actually pinning — that both kill paths
say the same sentence, and that the sweep drops password material — is unchanged.

**Decisions taken, with reasons.**

* **M2's `locked` is a new error code.** `version_conflict` means "the document
  changed since you loaded it" and the client reloads on it; a busy lock is a
  transient conflict with different advice, so reusing it would have been a lie
  the UI acts on. §6's key-code list in the architecture doc is not extended
  here — that is a design-doc edit, out of this scope.
* **M4 ships a data migration, not just two columns.** Backfilling
  `completed_notified_at`/`source_appended_at` from `completed_at` for existing
  COMPLETED envelopes is what stops a future resume mailing every recipient a
  second copy of something they signed weeks ago.
* **L5 changes one meter, not all of them.** The fixed calendar windows in
  `limits.py` all permit a 2× boundary burst. That is tolerable for an hourly
  op budget and is not tolerable for the one meter standing between an attacker
  and a document password, so only the password meter now slides (six ten-second
  buckets; the effective window is 60–70 s). The others are left alone
  deliberately, because changing a meter changes what every test of it means.
* **L14 keeps account enumeration, and says so in the code.** Closing it means
  answering every signup with a success shape and mailing the existing account
  instead — which strands the person who is actually stuck, in a product whose
  premise is that you get your file out without an argument. It buys less than
  it looks (login already distinguishes the two cases) and the rate is bounded
  by a per-IP throttle that H1 made real. Written at the decision, not here, so
  the next person decides it again rather than discovering it.
* **M6 is not attempted.** See the Human review queue row below. The three
  comments that claimed layer 2 covered DNS rebinding are corrected — it matches
  on the URL string, so an ordinary-looking hostname passes it whatever it
  resolves to at the moment Chromium connects. An overclaim in a comment is
  worse than the gap it describes.

**What is not covered.** The e2e suite was not run for this change: it needs the
full stack driven by Playwright and the only spec touched is `phase-0.spec.ts`,
which loses its "Run demo job" click along with the button (L1). The assertion it
loses — that the async pipeline works — is covered for real by the page-operation
specs, and by `test_jobs.py`, where the pipeline smoke moved.

**What the adversarial review found.** Five reviewers over the branch diff
(regression, security, correctness, test-quality, contract), then one
independent skeptic per finding whose brief was to *refute* it. 30 filed, 3
survived — and the survival rate is the point: most of what a review of a fix
branch turns up is either pre-existing, unreachable, or already true.

The three that survived, all fixed here:

* **M2 defeated M4.** `doc_lock` fails closed now, and `finalize_sign_request`
  was the one caller with no job row to fail — the exception simply ended the
  task, and nothing else ever finalizes an envelope. A worker SIGKILLed while
  holding the `sign-{id}` lock leaves the key alive for the TTL, so the
  `acks_late` redelivery waits 120 s, cannot acquire, and raises: a
  fully-signed contract sits at `sent` with no certificate until
  `sign_expirations` mails everyone that it expired. It retries now, which is
  safe *because* M4 made the body re-runnable — the two findings are each
  other's precondition.
* **`revert_version` had no `except EngineError`.** It never needed one until
  this branch gave it two new raise sites (the lock and the page cap); without
  it both arrive as `engine_error: Revert failed: …`.
* **L10's retry guard was per component, not per document.** The workspace is
  reused across `/app/doc/:id`, so the second document in a session got no
  recovery. The skeptic rejected the reviewer's reproduction and supplied a
  better one — a mode toggle re-mounts the viewer with no preceding
  `HttpClient` call — which is the review working as intended.

**And one the review found that was not a code defect at all.** The first
commit's `git add -A` swept in `_to_delete/zenpdf-stage.tgz` — 7.4 MB
containing `infra/.env`, `key.pem` and `zenpdf-dev.p12`, i.e. precisely what
`.gitignore` exists to keep out. Nothing had been pushed, so the branch was
rewritten to drop the blob from all eighteen commits (verified per commit with
`git ls-tree`), the file was restored to the working tree untracked, and
`_to_delete/` is now ignored. Three of the five reviewers filed it
independently; none of them was looking for it.

**Fixed while the review was still running**, because two reviewers each named
them and they were cheap: the metered-op charge moved *inside* the concurrency
slot (a race loser was paying an hour-window quota for a job that is never
created); `ViewerFacade.load()` gained a generation guard (the new error path
clears state, so a slow failure for the document you just left would wipe the
one you are looking at); the lock TTL narrowed to the op's actual lane (holding
every lock for the heavy ceiling means a crashed 60-second render wedges its
document for sixteen minutes); L5's window test rewritten to seed an *older
in-window* bucket, because filling only the newest one passes against the fixed
window it was supposed to refute; and `infra/perf/locustfile.py` stopped
seeding its job poll from the route L1 deleted.

**Refuted, and worth writing down.** L8's remaining five workspace panels are
out of scope — the branch never touched them. The NAT64 alternative's
non-canonical spellings are unreachable through layer 2. The password meter's
window is 50–60 s rather than the 60–70 s its comment claimed: the arithmetic
in the finding was right and the comment is corrected, but stricter-than-stated
is the safe direction for that particular meter.

**Every finding, what changed, and what proves it.**
